Of course, we also need to take care of children who get across again and again, there are special quotas so that we can take children out or care for them in an inclusive way. ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e6999	We're now on the 1st. Agenda item. We are going into the debate. First, Mr. Klubobmann will receive Mag. Arbitrator the floor. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e7026	Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen! The topic “Reform of Investigation Committees” has been considered in the media in recent days and in the reports of the individual politicians with some superlatives such as “breakthrough”, “historic day”, “great day”, “good day for parliamentarianism”. – With whatever name you want to classify this topic: In any case, this is an enrichment and a necessary and correct development of parliamentary democracy. At the beginning of this legislative period about a year ago, when all the initial decisions were taken, we also discussed: How can we make Parliament lively, interesting and at the same time targeted? How can we try to live a new parliamentaryism and also put it into structures? Discussions and the reform of the Committee of Inquiry, from minority law to the question of how the procedures in the Committee of Inquiry can be fairly and better arranged are essential in this context. I am also saying this because, for my group and for my party, the SPÖ, I also claim that at the beginning of this process we have come to know about this process of reform and that we have also kept talking about the current legislative proposal. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] The three laws that exist essentially include a minority right: A quarter of the Members of this House may request a committee of inquiry to determine the subject matter and the main key points of the procedure. By means of evidence decisions, they can determine which files are to be submitted and which persons to load and question as information persons. At the same time, however, we have also learned from the experience and differing views of the last committees of inquiry and have agreed on essential points regarding the development of the procedural provisions and the course of the examination committees, namely the choice of a closed, clearly defined issue, on a time-limited basis, on how long a committee of inquiry can take with extension possibilities, and on a targeted procedure in the survey and in the discussion. We have eliminated the possibility of judicial surveys and have made sure that the investigative committee serves what is also the goal of getting closer to the truth and responsibility. For the first time in this House, we have established an information system which is important not only for the committee of inquiry, but also for all the other procedures in Parliament. In this way, the Austrian Parliament takes the possibility of assessing whether a document or a matter is confidential, restricted or strictly secret, the administration at least partially out of hand, for at the end of the day the Austrian Parliament can decide which classification of each document to have. Of course, its classification of stone must be carried out by the administration, and that is the case. What has been discussed in the past, namely that a ministry has blackened its records in respect of the interests of the protection and that Parliament has considered that blackened acts do not allow the information work to be carried out, but in this way will no longer be a subject in the future, as the information regulation will enable all information to be made available to Parliament in the future, in compliance with this rule. It was discussed for a longer time, but you have to say: We are pioneers in Europe. Apart from Germany, no other European democracy knows similar rules. And I'd even say, Our rules go even further than the German rules. To sum up and conclude, what is to be said in this context: Firstly, I would like to thank you for the fact that the negotiation between five parliamentary parties is of course not easy. There are five different interests, five different experiences and five different views on the subject, and sometimes there are different assessments within the political groups. But at the end of the day we managed to put forward a proposal for a law here, which – unfortunately, as I believe, the Stronach team did not even sufficiently appreciate and understand the basic content of this proposal! – all five other parties can say: It's a good design. I would also like to thank the negotiators of the various parties, the club members, the colleagues of ÖVP, FPÖ, Greens and NEOS, Mr Pilz, Brosz, Darmann, August Wöginger, Lopatka and Pendl, so that we could advance this topic so well. Now it is my wish and hope that they will live up to what we want to do, because we really live a new Parliament, in which there will be information instead of scandalism and problem-solving approaches in the future, and in which we are working hard in the matter, but also fairly in dealing with the areas in which there are weaknesses in this republic and questionable questions to examine them fairly. The goal is always to ensure that Parliament is not a courtroom or courtroom, but that Parliament has political responsibility to clarify, so that in the future there is experience to prevent mistakes from being made again. It should then be possible that when we make laws, we respond to the mistakes of the past in such a way that there will be better laws in the future. That is the idea of committee of inquiry, and I would like to say in this sense. In any case, there is a very, very good draft here. - Thank you.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e7112	Mr Klubobmann Dr. Lopatka is now speaking. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e7133	My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Yes, the investigative committee is a milestone in Austrian parliamentarianism. This was also commented, partly even euphorically. I am always very careful in this regard and generally think that euphoria is not appropriate. But if I make a sober analysis of what is available here, I also have a positive result. I think we should be pleased together that what has already been tried in the last legislative period is now possible, and I am very sorry that not all parties agree to this regulation! This would have deserved the approval of all the parties represented here in Parliament! What did we want? – If the minority has this right to be able to set up a committee of inquiry – and we stand for that – then, of course, we must do everything we can to ensure that the procedure is justified in such a way that, when a committee of inquiry is set up, even those persons who have to declare themselves to be interested in such a committee of inquiry are protected accordingly. We deliberately wanted to turn this away from a political tribunal and now see in the regulations that exist the chance that we can actually achieve a new quality for these investigation committees. However, we also have a stronger legal basis in the Committee of Inquiry when it comes to resolving disputes between Parliament and the Government. The Constitutional Court is the body that comes to the train. He also enjoys public confidence when sensitive, controversial questions are to be resolved. Personally, it would have been best for the Presidency to have run an experienced legal personality, a procedural judge. But that was not possible. I am also satisfied with the current solution: that the three presidents who will be chairing the presidency are standing by a strong trial judge who is to be heard accordingly, who is making the initial question, who is at the end of the survey, who will draft the report, and who, therefore, is a guarantee that the procedure will be properly worked here. Nevertheless, there may be disputes. If the path to the Constitutional Court is taken, there will be a clear set of rules here for the first time, which will contribute to the fact that this political hack, which has often marked the investigation committees in the past, will no longer be on the agenda. It was important for us to clarify from the outset – and this has already been mentioned by Mr Schieder – that examination committees should often be used at a time when the courts are still in the middle of their work and that they are not hindered in their work. We have therefore also established a consultation mechanism in which the chairman has to conduct these consultations together with the trial judge in order to clarify when it comes to obtaining evidence, when it comes to informing people, so that we do not hinder the criminal justice of their activities in our work. It is also important that the investigative committee does not become a hub for strictly confidential documents, where overall public interests can also be threatened, including the lives of individuals working in very sensitive areas, when strictly confidential documents suddenly become public. It would therefore be very important for us to come to a new order here as well. We have achieved this with four levels of confidentiality with this information system now available, and on this point – very limited – I believe that the spread of immunity is justified. In other words, if a Member commits a secret betrayal, then appropriate criminal consequences are also possible, which were previously excluded. We have to make sure that there is a proper balance here. Name: Members should not be restricted in the matter of clarifying abuses. However, those who are in front of a committee of inquiry should not be confronted by a Member who can do and leave whatever he wants. [[Oh-noise of the Abg. Kogler.]] That is why it was important to us in the restricted case of slander – Mr Kogler, I think you will see it that a Member cannot hide behind his immunity if he slanders someone here. [[Abg. Kogler: Yes, eh!]] Therefore, it was important to us, limited – I repeat: limited – that immunity be broken when it comes to slander and mystery. I think that this momentary restriction makes sense. So can we be satisfied all around? – Yes, we can be satisfied, but I will not be really satisfied until the committee of inquiry works with us as the German colleagues have succeeded. For me, Germany is an example here, where Parliament is very responsible with the legal framework, which is very similar to the new legal framework that we have given ourselves. [[Abg. Brosz: It can only be seen if it is broadcast on TV!]] [[Intrusion of the Abg. Neubauer]] We need a change in political culture in some areas, namely: The Examining Committee should not be concerned with the scandalisation of the political opponent, but with the objective handling of abuses, in order to then lead to appropriate political consequences, in order to take legislative action to ensure that the Examining Committee does valuable work. It shouldn't be that we think we're a substitute court. No, we are not in a committee of inquiry. We are talking about political clarification and not criminal prosecution. To this end, the judicial authorities are much better called. I trust that, as we have succeeded in the negotiations, we will keep the general public interest in mind and that we will do so when the Committee of Inquiry is working. I assume that, because of the new legal situation, the opposition parties will, of course, benefit from this possibility – their good right – but the decisive thing is that we in this House will succeed in using this instrument properly. If this succeeds, not only has Parliament won, but also Austria. That should be our common interest! .. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of SPÖ and Greens]]

ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e7771	Mr Wöginger, you are next spoken. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e7792	Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen here in the National Council! Ladies and gentlemen on the gallery and at home in front of the screens! Yes, the reform of the Committee of Inquiry is certainly a milestone in the development of our parliamentaryism, and it should also be said that we really are among the models in Europe. We are the only country next to Germany that has a minority right installed here. We are here in the house – and that is what I would like to say – as regards speaking times as regards special actions concerning club status, so far very minority-friendly. And we are certainly among the most minority-friendly countries in Europe and all over the world with this reform. We have always said by the EIA, ladies and gentlemen, that we are saying yes to this minority right, but there must be an objective and objective process in the Committee, so there must be changes in the Rules of Procedure. That is, on the one hand, 46 Members may request an investigation committee in the future, but on the other hand, various measures will ensure an objective and objective process. We have always agreed in the negotiations that examination committees should no longer be as they were in the past. We now have a rule of law instead of a political show, a matter of information rather than a tribunal, and a proper rule of law is guaranteed in the future. And precisely these points are ensured by this reform: for example, an objective Presidency by the President, with the support of a well-known procedural judge, who can, of course, bring with it his long-standing competence. Another point is the rights of persons providing information by, for example, ensuring the initial survey by the court of procedure. It also sets out a special protection of media law – Mr. Strolz, that was particularly important to us – because we also knew from the past that at one time “normal” employees of any government office found themselves on the front page of a small-format daily newspaper the next day. In any case, we wanted to prevent this from happening again. There are also more competences for the lawyer who assists the persons before and during the survey. In the event of the violation of personal rights, you can also contact the Constitutional Court in the future. Furthermore, dealing with confidential documents is a matter that is particularly reflected in this package of laws. Clear rules of play linked to sanctions under the Information Regulation Act – that, I believe, is particularly important: on the one hand, we naturally get the documents that we need, but on the other hand, that there are clear rules on how to deal with them. There are no more ‘crout and beet committees’, but the subject matter of the investigation needs to be clearly identified and what can be dealt with at the end of the investigation committee. As a last point, I would like to address the separation of the examination committees from pre-election periods. It is now ensured that the final survey must take place at least 124 days before the election date when the legislative period expires regularly. That is in about four months, and this also ensures that we do not enter a pre-election period with an investigative committee, because it should serve the objective and objective educational work and not a political spectacle. Yes, we say yes to minority law, we say yes to an objective, objective and legally guaranteed procedure in committee. This is guaranteed by this package of laws. Finally, I should like to say thank you, first of all, to the Members of Parliament and the club who have played a key role here, in many hours, even countless, but above all also to the staff of the Parliamentary Directorate, but also to the clubs – and I would also like to stress this for the people here – who have managed to present a legislative package within a few weeks, which includes precisely these points, which also enable us to make good progress in parliamentarianism. Thank you very much to all those who have cooperated and helped that this package has now come to pass. And let us not lose sight of what we have jointly tabled today in the framework of a five-party proposal, so that it will work in the Committee as we have now agreed on this package. This is my wish when the first committee of inquiry begins to work. Thank you very much.

They are certified, but we have said that if we go to a new teaching practice, we also want to equip these teaching practices with high quality. That is to say, they will all be recertified, and the new ones will have to face up again because we want to prevent young colleagues from being trained in a teaching practice, but, I say now, they may also be exploited. We all have, I think, already experienced these things. Mr Franz also raised the question of training on the 15 most common diagnostics. I do not think he has read the law completely, that is, as far as training is concerned. We have made the 15 most common diagnostics in the nine months of basic education, which each doctor has to complete. This is, as it were, the beginning. It used to be like saying that you're doing surgery and internal. That's what many of us still know. Now it's the 15 most common diagnostics. That is, it divides into different subjects. There will be a lot of things that you, Mrs Mückstein, have also mentioned, because, of course, this also comes into the diagnosis. Dementia is certainly one of the most common diagnostics, and that is, also geriatry or neurology will be among these subjects. The training regulation, as it looks exactly, we are working, the house is currently working very intensively, and it will be followed as soon as possible. Mr Franz is not here now, I believe. He referred to the question of his application concerning the inclusion of patient representatives in the Independent Medicines Commission. I forgot to tell him: There's no longer any of this. That is why we did not agree to this request because it was abolished. It ended at the end of 2013. Perhaps one can then tell him that this request did not receive our approval because we did not want any patient representatives in this Commission, but because there is no longer in principle. So, I believe, the training of doctors is really an important step. Of course, we are trying to keep the young colleagues here on the spot with us. There is an important step in teaching practice. And with regard to the question of why the transitional periods are so long: We want to know whether we really find enough colleagues to train. We do not benefit if we now have a very short transitional period with the hours and over the years, but do not find any colleagues. That is why there should also be the possibility of taking part in Ambulatory or emergency medical departments, because we want colleagues not to wait, but that, if they enter this new training, they will be able to complete it as quickly as possible. – That would be my part of the medical law. On the question of Ebola. I think many of you have taken the media out of the way the course of Ebola is going. We know that we have had Ebola epidemics in different countries since 1976, which started in March 2014 and is certainly one that is very serious and, above all, very fast. We know – and this may also be good to communicate and good for you –: With Ebola you can only infect yourself – it has been strongly criticized by the liberals that I have said it is more dangerous to infect with flu or with measles than with Ebola, which is certainly true for Austria – if you have direct contact with an Ebolaful or deceased person, and you are infectious only if you are really ill. That is, what applies to flu or measles, namely, that in the incubation period already passed on by droplet infection viruses does not apply to Ebola. It is also not a disease that is transmitted by the respiratory tract, but really only through direct contact with blood, chair, vomiting. This means that the possibility of being infected in Austria is significantly lower at the moment than in the case of flu and all these things. Nevertheless, we know that due to travel activity there are, of course, possibilities for people who are ill with Ebola to leave in various countries. I was at an informal health meeting in Brussels last week to coordinate and, together with WHO and ECDC – which is the centre for communicable diseases in Europe – to clarify what measures are useful and necessary. We all have pictures in our head and all will have seen these pictures, where at airports people are measured the fever with a laser thermometer, i.e. fever controls are carried out. This may be a method that is visually very soothing. But we know, for example, of the case in Texas, that if someone has fever and fights this fever with drugs, he is naturally asymptomatic, that is to say, feverless, and also in the survey: Come from there, could you be sick?, perhaps not the truth says and that therefore entrance controls do not bring the 100% security. We also know that if one is to see that people coming on the road are being controlled, direct airports must be controlled. That is to say, in the European Union this applies to Great Britain, France and Brussels. On Thursday at this Health Minister's meeting, it was so that France and the UK had already made admission controls, i.e. Fieberthermometer and Questionnaires, and that after this meeting, Brussels decided to do the same. But the clear tenor of all was: If something really works effectively, it is checking exits, i.e. checks on exits from the countries concerned. It was decided that both WHO and independent observers would travel to the countries to see if this would work well. ECDC and WHO both reported at this meeting that checks – including independent checks – are carried out on-the-spot and that they can confirm that they really work well. Of course, there is no 100% security. What we have decided to do in Austria and what I have brought in is to rely on large-scale information. That is, we now have posters at all airports and large railway stations – because of course there is also the possibility that someone will fly to Munich and then travel by train to Austria – hang posters, posters in three languages, which not only provide information in case someone has Ebola or might have infected, but also information about MERS. All of you may have been persecuting that we already have a case of MERS here in Austria and that there might also be more to come. We have therefore placed posters here with information on the question: Where do you turn if you feel you're sick? If you feel you're sick, it's very important: Call the doctor, call the rescue, go nowhere! If you were in Sierra Leone, were in Guinea, came back and could have been in contact with an Ebola sufferer, it is important to try to do this by phone. If you come from there and worry that you have the symptoms and could be sick, you should not go into large human collections, visit hospitals, ambulances or also the doctor. What my wish was and where the European Commission has said it will also try to achieve this somehow: that we should try to get the data network. We all know that people who come to Austria from these countries need visas. We know, for example, that Vienna Airport had six million passengers in the last half of the year. 160 of these – we know this because of the visa data – came from the countries concerned. That means it's a really small number that landed with us via second airports. And we know that likely many of these people who have landed with us have come to us as part of their UN activity – we are UN seats, and many people come to us as part of the UN. This means that the travel activities we have to expect here are relatively small. Nevertheless, it would be wise to have as many data as possible, for example in the question of where I can be reached for the next three weeks, on the one hand if a case really occurred on the plane and there would have been a contact with a patient, on the other hand if one wanted to question, the one is still in the country, how is it going or is something going on? The European Commission has said that it will try to establish a database in this regard. In Austria we have set up a hotline, which can be reached from 7 a.m. to 10 p.m. This hotline has been set up for a long time. We have about 200 to 300 calls in the time of their establishment, which is almost a three-quarters year. Most calls refer to the question of travel activity, i.e. I can go to a particular area or not, and very few to the question of Ebola and Ebola disease. I suggested a so-called Task Force and I will also call it in the next week, because I believe it is important – as most European countries do and as the European Union does – at the highest political level, at the level of the ministries concerned – that it will be the BMI, the BMG, and the BMVIT because of the travel activities – to have a coordinated group together with the airport and the rescue services. We will also try to find a coordinator to coordinate the measures. These measures are also concerned with: How do I get to the beds I need for the care? In this context, the question of the laboratories was also raised yesterday. Mrs Belakovich said we have no level 4 lab. That's right. Level 4 laboratories are designed for the research purposes with Ebola, but for the diagnostics of Ebola only Level 2, and we have a Level 3 laboratory with the AGES laboratory. That is, there is no reason to worry, because we are even a level higher than what is required. We currently have two beds, possibly a third bed in Vienna, and we have already found in the federal states with a coordination meeting that there are also possibilities there – in Tyrol, for example – to include suspect cases, on the one hand, and on the other hand cases that may also be affected. At the same time, we also coordinate, as has already been said, not only through information on posters, but also on the question of beds, measures with regard to MERS. It must also be clear that the patient's transport and all these things are clear and regulated. The European Union is currently trying to provide aircraft because the patients who have so far come to the European Union with Ebola were helpers who have been infected there and who have fled back. To do this, I'm just trying to draw a picture: The plane, which flies back to Ebola, is a completely de-cored plane, that is, it's completely empty inside, and the German Minister of Health told me that the disinfection of this aircraft lasts for a week. That means you have to assume that more than one aircraft is necessary. Germany has an aircraft, the European Union is supposed to buy one more, and we are also coordinating with the USA that there can still be an exchange. With us, the staff – especially the staff in the Kaiser-Franz-Josef-Spital – are intensively trained those who are already in charge of the MERS case and who are really in high-level training. We know you're protected from infection only if you're really well trained. It does not use the best protective suit if, for example, improper dress and dress up or a mistake, a fault happens when it comes to the question of undressing. There is therefore massive training. What we are relying on is to inform the people together with the doctor's chamber, together with the Austrian Health and Medical Association. We will also try to approach kindergartens, schools, i.e. teachers, with information on the question: Do I really have to fear Ebola? We all know the stories. I can tell you a story: A friend who has a chemotherapy and has to go out with a mouth protection to avoid infecting himself was rejected by three taxi drivers because they said she could have Ebola. In other words, we will really inform the people in a comprehensive way in order to avoid panic or excessive reactions, as has been said. This is the way Austria is going. The sage “Panik is also an epidemic”, which, I think, we should really write in the main book. I believe that in Austria, it is also very, very good to be really well informed about the media. I have decided to carry out a very open communication with my department in this field, i.e. to say very openly and very clearly what is the matter, because I believe that the Austrians have really deserved this, and we also see that, as I mean, we are doing very well with it. [[Complementary of the Abg. Muttons.]] We are prepared, but nevertheless: Let's keep looking at it every day so that we don't somehow get into the backlog! – Thank you many times for your attention.

I would now like to focus on why this is so important, namely: According to WHO, Ebola is among the so-called Biosafety Level L4, which means that you need an L4 laboratory and also a L4 station for the reception of highly infectious patients, but both are missing in Austria. This means that Ebola samples must be transferred to Germany to the Bernhard-Night-Institute in Hamburg. Of course, the problem of sample transport is to be taken into account, as according to WHO Directive, the cross-border air transport of patients suspected of having a highly infectious disease is not permitted, which must also be carried out on samples. Transport therefore takes place by land, which takes precious time. All the plans of the past – that is now my criticism point – to set up an L4 laboratory and a L4 bed station in Austria and especially in Vienna for the reception of highly infectious patients have failed the inability of the federal and state states. At the time, the Federal Government was not prepared to allocate the small financial resources for the establishment and operation. We now rely on the benevolence of foreign laboratories as regards the diagnosis, and improvise in stationary accommodation, which, of course, represents a huge risk potential. – I said it yesterday: Of course, it's true that we're going to get over the rounds in one, two, five cases, but if it's going to be more, then we'll get massive trouble. That makes the doctor's chamber together with any institutes, but it will be verified. This means that many small hospitals would no longer have gymnastics if they were. That is why it has been said that a gymnastics doctor should be employed for 15 patients, but this job is not used for training purposes, i.e. it is not counted. What does that mean? – If we turned the number of beds we have today to this key, we would have far too few doctors to care for these patients. That is to say, there is something to do with this derogation. This will be a huge bottleneck, ladies and gentlemen, which will massively affect young colleagues in their training as a practical doctor. This will, of course, lead in further consequence to their whistles to make the training in Austria and will only be quite exodus. That's a warning I'm saying here. I'm just saying we have to try not to build up any obstacles! We must not produce headlines, make well-intentioned laws, which then become a disaster in the formulation! I do not want to become a caller who conjures up disasters here, but if it actually comes about that we only have about 10 percent training for gymnastics here and we would actually need much more in a recognized training centre, then this is a mere disaster and the colleagues will emigrate. That is why, ladies and gentlemen, it is unfortunate that the reform unfortunately ensures that young doctors can continue to be used everywhere, but only longer as system holders – in Vienna we say “the syringe ferdln” – and an improvement in training will not be achieved. It is being sought, but unfortunately not achieved. I'm trying to work through this again in the narrow circle, otherwise in Austria we have an blatant medical shortage in a very short time.

ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e11268	The next speaker is Mr Wöginger. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e11291	Madam President. Members of the Federal Government! Ladies and gentlemen! This fits very well with the previous item on the agenda, on medical training, and I join my colleagues Erwin Rasinger: It is another small but very important step towards a positive development of one of the best health systems worldwide. We also see very well here, ladies and gentlemen, how closely the social issues are linked to the health sector. A fair balance must always be found between hospitals and staff on the one hand and patients on the other, which can also be financed. Shorter working hours for doctors are, of course, an attraction for this professional group. However, it is also a significant improvement in the sense of patients. What is the substance in some points? – It is gradually reduced the average weekly working time to 48 hours, in three steps, if you want to: primarily once to 60 hours until 2017, then until 2021 to 55 hours and from 1. ==References====External links== And then we also have an annual calculation of 52 weeks, before 26 weeks – that is, a half-yearly calculation. Yes, in exceptional situations or especially seasonally due to certain hospitals – I am thinking of ski areas and the like – it will be possible for doctors to work 72 hours a week in a few weeks even in the next few years. The second point is the term of service. For extended services it is also reduced in stages to 25 hours, again in these three steps: Only on 32 hours – weekends and holidays are taken into account here with a maximum of 49 hours – in a second step from 2018 to 2021 to 29 hours, and from 1. ==References====External links== The compensation period must be taken immediately after the extended service and cannot be paid financially. For weekly working hours and length of service – i.e. for the reduction in this area – both an operating agreement and the signature of the person concerned, the doctor, the doctor in question are required. ÖGB, the Chamber of Commerce and the Chamber of Physicians expressly agree with this law. We keep the social partners high. That is to say, it has really been properly negotiated in this area. The countries have been addressed. Of course, some countries can live very well with it, some countries have this compromise. Without advocating this, all the countries have this agreement, Mrs Belakovich-Jenewein. And it is a viable compromise with which the countries as a whole can also live. We still have an open point, Mrs Bundesminister, and I would like to address it here – as an above Austrian representative, as an employee of the Red Cross and also as a works council: We have an excellent emergency medical system in Austria. I really want to stress this. Wherever you need an emergency doctor in this country, the emergency period is given within 10 minutes, whether it's brought with an operational vehicle or by helicopter. This is a great achievement that we have in our Republic. It is now a question of whether the hours of emergency work and also of teaching and research activities in the future are included in this maximum working time. ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e14023	Madam President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! First of all, I would like to point out in principle that a Federal Minister of the Republic is committed to the Constitution and I therefore have to comply with the applicable rights and laws. Since the law concerning the hypo has not yet been repealed, it is in force and valid, and therefore I do not have to distance myself from it, but rather to deal with it in accordance with the constitution. It has nothing to do with whether I am for or against this law. I therefore ask you to take note of this in such a way that it is already a crucial question for this rule of law that I, as the Federal Minister of State, adhere to existing laws. My personal opinion on this is quite different. In response to the question raised in this brief debate, I can comment as follows: The reply to the debate, 2078/AB, deals with the question on 1 January. In August 2014, the Federal Law on Rehabilitation Measures entered into force for Hypo Alpe-Adria-Bank International AG, in short called HaaSanG. In the interests of Austrian taxpayers, this law allows the participation of the old owners as well as of subordinate believers in the costs of the settlement of the Hypo Alpe-Adria bank. . I will briefly inform you about the ongoing process in the course of the response to the request. In particular, a contribution by the Bayerische Landesbank was necessary due to its previous majority participation in the bank saved by the emergency state in 2009, which was initiated by my predecessor in close coordination with the Ministry of Justice in the HaaSanG Act under discussion. Questions 1 and 3: ==References====External links== On 3 March, the decision to participate in the reconstruction of the Hypo Alpe-Adria was also taken. The debt was recorded by all subordinated bonds, which had a maturity date prior to the 1st century. ==References== This deadline was therefore chosen because experience has shown that the time factor – about five years before the completion of the renovation – could indeed be more sufficient. A time limit on rehabilitation obligations is required in order to minimise your criticised severity of intervention in legal positions. The volume of subordinate liabilities affected by HaaSanG thus includes around €800 million. A first estimate of the bank was based on a slightly higher volume. On question 2: Since the Republic of Austria has already spent around 5.5 billion on the Hypo Alpe-Adria – of which around 4.3 billion in the form of capital grants – it was the right decision from the Federal Ministry of Finance that, in particular, those who held a dominant stake in the Hypo Alpes-Adria at the time, or increased yields on other creditors and bondsigners, had to pay. In this context, it is essential that the subordinate financial instruments to meet regulatory own resources requirements have been issued and recognised by the supervisory authority. Questions 4 to 7: The German bond issued to strengthen the own resources situation in December 2012 with a maturity date of 1 billion euros and subordinated bonds in the double-digit million range after 1st year. Therefore, June 2019 is not subject to the HaaSanG. HaaSanG's subordinate bonds or bonds are set out in the regulation in the BGBl. II No 195/2014 published. That is about 60 emissions, so I would like to ask you to understand – as all this is published – that I do not refer to all 60 again orally at this point. However, if Questions 4 to 7 address those subordinated bonds that are not covered by the HaaSanG because they have a maturity date after the 1st century. I cannot answer the question for the following reason: These bonds are traded in the secondary market and there are no publicly available Register for the holders of these bonds. Therefore, it is not possible to list these in detail, as required. Briefly to the current situation, resolution Hypo: Many steps have been taken. One step is that the mining company was founded. In the next few weeks, the total assets will be transferred to them. The tendering period for the appointment of the Executive Board has already expired. The Supervisory Boards have already been appointed, so that this new decommissioning company will no longer have a bank concession and you can actually start transfers of services and decommissioning from next month onwards. The second major subject area, which is still part of the hypo, is the area of sale of the SEA. This is the part that covers the countries of Serbia, Croatia, Montenegro and cetera. The final negotiations are already under way. We expect a decision over the next week on how to carry out these SEA transfers. It remains the Bank in Italy, which, in any case, has to be put to a halt under the agreements, so that, in this context, all measures are now in place and implemented so that the Hypo chapter can be completed – albeit inconveniently. It will cost us a lot of effort to get to a constructive path, to sell these around 18 billion assets that will now be included in the mine, so that for the Republic of Austria and thus for taxpayers the damage is as low as possible. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e18071	Mr President. Mr. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! In June of this year, we decided on the legal security of the film/TV agreement with a donation of € 8 million. This is now another legislative innovation that will facilitate the promotion of the Austrian film. Film projects can now be funded by up to 50 percent, small and commercially difficult films with up to 80 percent of production costs. In exceptional cases, in film projects of particular cultural interest, even government funding of more than 80 percent of the costs is possible. This innovation addresses the difference between commercial mass products and culturally demanding films that contribute to European culture and to regional diversity and identity. I still think that this innovation will also allow for film projects that would not be implemented at all without grants. This will create jobs on the one hand, but on the other hand it will also provide a range of opportunities for the Austrian public interest in terms of regionality and diversity. ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e19033	We're coming to the 20th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. First speaker: Mr Mag. Stefan. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e19060	Mr President. Mr. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! In short, only for the introduction: What's this about? – It is a matter of establishing in 1920 – at the time when the Constitution was decided here in Austria – that the borders of district courts should not overlap with the borders of political districts. The sense was that there should be some units that the citizen understands in which he is moving, so that he knows: Where is my District Court? dig? – Wherever my political district is! Everyone knows that; where his court is, perhaps not at the first moment, but which district he is assigned, very well. What has happened in recent years? – District courts were grouped together, for it was written on the flags to put together district courts that are too small – “too small” means less than four judges per court. There are now or there were 82 district courts that should be reduced to 35; now there are 56 to be reduced. In the course of this, district courts were also brought together in such a way that there was an overlap with the political districts. We have pointed out this and have said: That is unconstitutional, and such a decision is simply and simply not to be taken! Nor can a regulation be adopted, nor can it be adopted, if it is unconstitutional! This was deliberately taken into account – and, of course, the Constitutional Court of Justice has also been called upon by those concerned, which has led to a period of legal uncertainty, or is still in existence, in which it is unclear whether or not decisions by courts that are in fact not responsible have been taken. So it's a very unpleasant state! The Constitutional Court has therefore also imposed a repair period here. That is once the beginning and the state that was consciously made by this federal government and by the government parties. So you didn't go here and you said: Well, we note that this is not possible because of the Constitution, so we must first have talks, change the Constitution and then make this merger! – One has chosen the other way. That is totally unreasonable! [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] The actual aggregation of the District Courts is not a reputation, either, because if you look at the Court of Auditors' report, which is quite fresh, you can see that there has been a great deal of failure here. And that's... Not really precise concepts have been developed, but some district courts have been officially combined, but because in reality this merger did not work spatially, secondary offices were then carried on at the old courts. This has led to a complete uncertainty on the part of those responsible, the judges who have to go back and forth, and so on. That is not, as has been said, a leaf of fame. The next point to be noted here: We only changed the rule of law yesterday or amended a decision in this respect. This is a question of the jurisdiction of district courts. There are value limits, and this value limit has been increased to €15 000 in the past few years, because, because of inflation, the amounts are changing again and again, so that the district courts are properly burdened and not too much goes to the nearest courts. It has been proposed that these amounts should be increased in the next few years. Now you've gone here and said: No, no, we do not raise them any further, because the district courts are fully burdened! – But that contradicts what I have just said: that they say the district courts are too small, they have too little to do, so we have to resolve them. That is to say, one deliberately makes decisions here to stop assigning files to the district courts so that they can then be dissolved. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] Therefore, our rejection – and in this context also the behaviour in Styria, if you look at how communities are grouped together without questioning the population there, although expressly a stock guarantee for municipalities was also laid down in the Government Convention, namely the inventory guarantee for municipalities and cities, to carry out mergers only after mandatory referendums. This has been completely negated here again, and this also shows how the population is being dealt with. Therefore, I make the following request: [[Quoted print version removed]] So it would be time, in fact, to involve the population in the decisions and not to go over them again.

ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e20625	Mr Krainer is next spoken. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e20647	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! What's this about? So far, indirect banking recapitalisation has been possible – it has also happened, for Spain. The disadvantage of this is, in principle, the following: If a country itself does not have enough money or is unable to borrow enough money on the international capital market to recapitalise its own banks – not because it is a lot of fun, but because it is simply necessary in specific situations – then it has been possible so far that this country is getting the money over the ESM, but this sum is almost uprooted to the country in debt. That means that it makes it difficult for the country to refinance itself. What we're doing now is this: If a country does not have enough money, but it is nevertheless necessary to carry out a recapitalisation of banks there, the money needed will no longer be charged to the debt of the country. Otherwise, they'd be able to borrow it themselves. So far, a little bit of the child has been poured out with the bath. This means that the rescue instruments are improved and refined and we assume that we no longer need them at all. There is currently no case, and I do not see anyone coming to us either. But you do not create instruments for rescue because you already know what is happening, but so that you have the necessary instruments when something happens. And here the rescue instrument is refined. With regard to Mr Kogler's question concerning parliamentary rights of participation: In principle, we have already discussed this in committee. We believe that, in such a case, it must go through plenary and then go through the committee with certain documents. But we are very happy to have discussions between the political groups, whether one or one of them is going to be the agreement, I think, to be fulfilled for a year and a half, or whether there should be any improvements. That is what we have offered in the Committee, and this offer, of course, goes on. - Thank you.

The key elements of the Banking Union had to be implemented. And then we found an instrument that works as part of the ESM and not as a new instrument, which is limited to 60 billion across Europe. This means that we do not assume any additional liability here, but that what was decided in the National Council also has this validity. It is therefore necessary that I, as governor of the ESM, be empowered to agree to this new instrument. The Financial Committee discussed in detail the importance of this empowerment. I would therefore like to point out once again the following, which is particularly dear to me: Direct banking aid has extremely strict requirements. Only banks under the central supervision of the ECB can be directly helped. There is an absolute issue of unanimity for the application of this instrument. It is therefore not possible to agree on the adoption of this instrument either. Each bank included in this new instrument shall be subject to the most accurate assessments. Only viable banks – as I am back with you if they are not viable – are currently receiving resolution instruments – receive direct ESM support, and EU aid law has to be respected 100%. Therefore, even if banks are directly capitalised, the State remains in its responsibility, because only the State can claim that these funds will be paid out at all. In the end, this means that it is clear and unmistakable – and this was also stated in the discussion we had in the Financial Committee – that the National Council needs to be re-examined in each application. That is so, and that is clearly the case. It is crucial that these public funds can only flow in the extreme emergency. The ESM is, as it were, the end of the long chain, not the beginning of a process. Only after a system-related bank has tried to raise capital over the market until the owners of the bank and the creditors have been integrated – keyword: Bail-in – and only if the Member State concerned is unable to meet the remaining needs without even getting into serious problems, will this new ESM instrument be available as the last means. This new instrument for direct recapitalisation requires no change in our legal framework. In the event of an application for direct recapitalisation of the grant of grants, the National Council must agree. As with all other ESM assistance, the National Council must agree to the political requirements of the ESM Member State and the same rules as those applicable to state aid will apply. Therefore: If you today agree that I can vote in the Board of Governors for Austria and agree to the ESM instrument, then don't hand me a blank check, but you will be dealt with again and again in individual cases.

ParlaMint-AT_2014-10-23-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00046_d8e22767	Madam President. In recent times, in the media, we have been able to pursue a certain campaign against all these contracts that are being concluded with the North American region. And by chance – almost as if it were staged – I was able to read in a daily newspaper today: ‘FATCA: New tax agreements with the USA create excitement”, Tyrolean does not receive life insurance. What about FATCA? – It is an agreement with the USA, which is actually applicable in 2014, and now the ratification is to take place. If you look at that exactly, you see: It is nothing but a one-way street that has been created on pressure from the USA because we commit ourselves to inform the U.S. tax authorities about the account movements of all account holders with US reference – whether it be US citizens or other persons who have accounts in Austria – or about what they have deposited in money. If that is not the case, then the US will have the opportunity to retain 30 percent withholding tax. However, if a financial institution does not participate, it will lose access to the US market under this FATCA agreement. So that's where the Americans exerted quite a lot of pressure. I therefore say that it is a one-way road because vice versa it is not guaranteed that we get information from America. There is even the possibility for the American Finance Office to make group requests to the Austrian tax authorities, with the request to obtain the refused data and information by means of an official assistance procedure. That means there's no escape. Somehow this makes me think that the tax authorities are probably working with the NSA, which already know for a long time what amounts are on the accounts in Austria. This means nothing more than that bank secrecy is no longer applicable to US-related account holders; this has now been definitely done. In addition, our banks also have an additional burden, namely in the IT sector, of approximately €100 million to book. Austria has, I mentioned, no rights in return. Austria had to give up the banking secret completely, and we can make sure that we ever get data from the USA. In this way, this agreement is absolutely unacceptable.
